⎿  Updated skill/claude-call/VOICE.md (+3 -1)
     2  
     3  You are Claude on a live voice call with one person, under the 
        name given above. Work exactly as you normally would as a codin
        g agent: read, search, run commands, edit files when asked. The
         only difference is how you talk. Everything you want the perso
        n to hear goes inside `<say>` tags, and it is read aloud by a t
        ext to speech voice the moment you write it.
     4  
     5 -Every user message starts with a tag such as `[call · visual+au
       -dio · you are on the line]`. If the person cut in on you, a `[c
       -ut in: …]` note says what they heard and what they missed.     
     5 +The first message starts with a tag such as `[call · visual+aud
       +io · you are on the line]`; it comes again only when it changes
       +, so the last one you saw still holds. If the person cut in on 
       +you, a `[cut in: …]` note says what they heard and what they mi
       +ssed.                                                          
     6 +                                                               
     7 +If this conversation is ever summarised to make room, keep the 
       +work: what you were asked, decisions made, files changed, what 
       +is left and anything you are waiting on the person for. Leave o
       +ut the call itself — tags, cut-ins, fillers, who was on the lin
       +e.                                                             
     8  
     9  The tag also says whether you are **on the line** or **in the b
        ackground**. On the line, your `<say>` text is spoken aloud in 
        your own voice, right away. In the background it is not spoken 
        at all: the central assistant reads out a one-line summary inst
        ead. So when you are in the background, say less, and make the 
        last thing you say a single sentence that works on its own as a
         report — it is the only part the person will hear.

Ran 2 shell commands
Yes, the gap between the text and the voice really did get bigger, and it was my doing. I've fixed that and made the context changes you asked for. All 187 tests pass, but I haven't heard any of this on a live call yet. Voice lagging behind the text "Stereo voices" was on by default. With it on, the server makes every sentence as audio using macOS say, which takes 2–2.5 seconds per sentence on this machine. The text showed up straight away while the voice trailed behind. Before that, the browser spoke instantly. Now, if a sentence's audio isn't ready within 0.35 s, the browser says it instead. It's the same Mac voice, just from the centre rather than the agent's side. The server also starts making the next few sentences while the current one plays, so later ones are more likely to come out panned. If you'd rather have no gap and no panning at all, turn off Stereo voices in Settings. Context: different for Ada and for the agents Before, two-thirds of every message Ada got was the same full team list, repeated each turn. Her conversation also started from scratch on every server restart, so she forgot everything, including what mattered. Ada: - Only changes: she gets the full team once at the start. After that she only gets [team changes since your last message: …], or nothing if nothing changed. She gets the full list again every 15 turns, and right after Claude Code summarises (compacts) her conversation. - Fresh conversation on each server start: her day-to-day chatter ("go to Juno", "pause") isn't carried forward. What matters is kept in a notebook, ~/buddy-home/.buddy/ada-notebook.json, which she is shown at the start of each conversation. It holds: - things you asked her to remember (she now has <remember> and <forget> tags), such as "we went with Postgres" - each project's latest report, one line per project - recent events, logged automatically: project set up or opened, work handed out, project closed Project agents: - They keep their full sessions, because their history is the actual work. - The call header is now sent only when it changes, not on every turn. - Their prompt now says that if their conversation is summarised, they should keep decisions, files changed and open questions, and drop the call chatter. The main changes are in the new server/notebook.ts and in server/call.ts, with tests in server/notebook.test.ts. To try it: the server running on port 3000 (pid 51029, the one you started) is still on the old code. Restart it to pick up these changes, then reload the page. Nothing is committed yet.

Running the full test suite one last time, then stopping my test server and committing:
Ran 2 shell commands
ClaudeCall can now be installed as an app on a phone, iPad or laptop, and run in the background on macOS or Linux. Everything is committed locally in two commits, nothing pushed, both authored by Palak: - 552c96a: all the earlier work, including the voice-gap fix and Ada's notebook - a1f85c1: the deployment work Your more_features.txt notes are left out. All 188 tests pass. App on phone, iPad and laptop - It can be added to the home screen or dock and opens full screen with its own icon. If the computer is asleep or unreachable, it shows a "can't reach your computer" page instead of a browser error. - Layout: I took screenshots at 8 sizes, from phone to laptop. Nothing scrolls sideways and the page shows no errors. - Phone, iPad held upright and small windows: one section at a time, with tabs at the bottom. - Phone on its side: the call card goes side by side, so the controls fit without scrolling. - Small laptop and iPad landscape: two columns, the call on the left and the canvas on the right. - The "Choose folder" button now only appears on the computer running ClaudeCall. On a phone it would have opened Finder on your laptop. macOS and Linux - Background service: npm run service install sets it up (add -- --lan for phones on your Wi-Fi). It starts at login, restarts if it stops, and keeps the same access code. It also has status, logs, restart and uninstall. - Linux: I checked it in a clean Linux container. It installed, the tests passed, the server started, speech recognition loaded and the page and connection worked. Stereo voices and "Test how it's heard" use macOS's own voices, so they're hidden on Linux. Phones: the README explains two ways to get the secure address the microphone needs: - Tailscale (recommended): a real certificate, and it works away from home. You run tailscale serve --bg 3000 on the computer. - Same Wi-Fi: a certificate ClaudeCall makes itself, accepted once on each device. Not tested yet: - I didn't run the service install on your Mac, because it would clash with the server you're running on port 3000. Linux was only tested in a container, without systemd or a real claude. - I haven't tried it on a real iPhone or iPad. To try it, stop your current server (it's still on the old code) and run npm run service install -- --lan. It prints the address and code to open on your phone. I can do that step for you if you want.

    106 +# a failed attempt can leave the half-made image attached: let
        + it go before the next try                                    
    107 +let_go() {                                                    
    108 +  hdiutil info | awk -v img="$OUT" '$1 == "image-path" { on = 
        +($3 == img) } on && /^\/dev\/disk[0-9]+\t/ { print $1 }' \    
    109 +    | while read -r dev; do hdiutil detach "$dev" -force >/dev
        +/null 2>&1 || true; done                                      
    110 +  rm -f "$OUT"                                                
    111 +}                                                             
    112 +made=0                                                        
    113  if diskutil image create from --help >/dev/null 2>&1; then
    107 -  diskutil image create from --format UDZO --volumeName Claude

    113 -  done                                                        
    114 +  diskutil image create from --format UDZO --volumeName Claude
        +Call "$STAGE" "$OUT" >/dev/null 2>&1 && made=1 || let_go      
    115  fi
    116 +for try in 1 2 3 4 5; do                                      
    117 +  [ "$made" = 1 ] && break                                    
    118 +  hdiutil create -quiet -volname ClaudeCall -srcfolder "$STAGE

Turn 7

how can i do it via github actions, so that the dmg, linux image, app for pther phone ipad etc, are built there itself
Claude's response
GitHub Actions can build the Mac and Linux versions. Phones and iPads are different: the installable app there is the web app your computer serves, so there's nothing to build for them. Native App Store or Play Store apps would need developer accounts and signing keys, which is your decision; more on that at the end. To keep CI simple, I'll make each build script able to target either processor from one machine (Apple silicon and Intel for Mac, x64 and arm64 for Linux). ⎿  Created scripts/linux-launcher.sh (+23 -0)
     1 +#!/bin/sh                                                      
     2 +# "ClaudeCall" in the Linux app menu (and the `claudecall` comm
       +and): make sure the service is up,                             
     3 +# then open the page, already signed in with the access code. i
       +nstall.sh puts it in place.                                    
     4 +CONF="$HOME/.config/claudecall"                                
     5 +UNIT=${CLAUDECALL_LABEL:-claudecall}.service                   
     6 +get() { sed -n "s/^$1=//p" "$CONF/env" 2>/dev/null | tail -1 | 
       +sed 's/^"\(.*\)"$/\1/'; }                                      
     7 +PORT=$(get PORT); PORT=${PORT:-3000}                           
     8 +SCHEME=http                                                    
     9 +[ "$(get BUDDY_HOST)" = 0.0.0.0 ] && SCHEME=https              
    10 +up() { curl -ksf -o /dev/null "$SCHEME://localhost:$PORT/"; }  
    11 +                                                               
    12 +up || systemctl --user start "$UNIT" 2>/dev/null               
    13 +i=0                                                            
    14 +while ! up && [ $i -lt 60 ]; do sleep 0.5; i=$((i + 1)); done  
    15 +if ! up; then                                                  
    16 +  msg="ClaudeCall did not start. See: journalctl --user -u $UNI
       +T"                                                             
    17 +  command -v notify-send >/dev/null && notify-send "ClaudeCall"
       + "$msg"                                                        
    18 +  echo "$msg" >&2                                              
    19 +  exit 1                                                       
    20 +fi                                                             
    21 +# the code travels after the #, which never leaves the browser;
       + the page drops it at once                                     
    22 +[ -n "${CLAUDECALL_NO_OPEN:-}" ] && exit 0                     
    23 +xdg-open "$SCHEME://localhost:$PORT/#code=$(get BUDDY_TOKEN)" >
       +/dev/null 2>&1 &

The GitHub Actions workflow is set up and committed locally (62ef3bb, 86ee186). It hasn't run yet, because nothing has been pushed. What it does (.github/workflows/build.yml). Every push to main and every pull request runs the tests, then builds each download and checks it starts, including speech recognition loading:
┌────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────┬──────────────────────┐ │ Download │ Built │ Start-up check │ │ │ on │ │ ├────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────┤ │ ClaudeCall-<ver>-mac-apple-silicon.dmg │ macOS │ run on the runner │ │ │ runner │ │ ├────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────┤ │ ClaudeCall-<ver>-linux-x64.tar.gz │ Linux │ run on the runner │ │ │ runner │ │ ├────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────┤ │ ClaudeCall-<ver>-linux-arm64.tar.gz │ Linux │ run in an emulated │ │ │ runner │ ARM container │ ├────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────┤ │ │ Linux │ APK inspected for │ │ ClaudeCall-<ver>-android.apk │ runner │ the microphone │ │ │ │ permission │ └────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────┴──────────────────────┘
The files can be downloaded from each run's page. Pushing a version tag also publishes them as a GitHub Release, with install notes: git push origin main git tag v0.2.0 && git push origin v0.2.0 How well it's tested - Mac and Linux builds: both built here. The Mac app passed the start-up check, including speech recognition. - Linux packages: not run here, because Docker on your Mac is stopped. CI runs them. - Linux install.sh: never run anywhere. It needs systemd, which CI doesn't have. - Android: never built here. As you chose, its first build happens on GitHub, so expect a fix-up round if anything fails. Android app. It asks once for your computer's address and access code, then opens ClaudeCall full screen with the microphone allowed and the screen kept on. On your Wi-Fi it asks once whether to trust your computer's own certificate. To make that easy, ClaudeCall's Settings → Phones and tablets now shows the address and code to type in. Two Android limits: - Microphone with the Wi-Fi certificate untested. The microphone might not work in the app over the Wi-Fi setup's self-made certificate. Tailscale gives a real certificate, so it's the safer route. - Updates won't install over an older version until you add a signing key as a repository secret. The README has the four steps. Things that changed or still need deciding - No Intel Mac app. The speech recogniser's runtime dropped Intel Macs, so the Mac app is Apple silicon only. An Intel build now stops with a clear error. - iPhone/iPad stay a web app (Add to Home Screen). A real iOS app needs a $99/year Apple developer account to install at all. - Mac app signing. The DMG still says "unidentified developer" until you sign it with an Apple Developer ID, which needs that same account. - Pushing. The remote is palakraisinghani/TalkToClaude, and pushing earlier was waiting on Palak's access. Tell me if you want me to push.

    30  
    31  - `<voice to="juno" name="Karen" speed="1.1" volume="0.8" side=
        "left"/>` — how an agent sounds: `name` is a system voice (Dani
        el, Karen, Moira, Rishi, Tara, Samantha…), `speed` a number aro
        und 1 or "faster"/"slower", `volume` 0 to 1 or "louder"/"quiete
        r", `side` left, right or centre, `pitch` "higher"/"lower". Lea
        ve out `to` to change your own voice. Give only what was asked 
        for.
    32  - `<setting name="chimes" value="off"/>` — a setting on the lea
        d's page: `chimes` (an agent's tone before it speaks), `ring` (
        the chime when someone needs them), `stereo` (voices placed lef
        t and right), `audio-only`, `cleanup` (tidying what they say be
        fore it reaches an agent), `hold-calls`. Values are on or off.
    33 +- `<control to="juno" do="mode" value="plan"/>` — Claude Code's
       + controls for an agent (leave out `to` for yourself): `do="mode
       +"` with `value` auto, plan, acceptEdits or manual (ask every ti
       +me); `do="effort"` with low, medium, high, xhigh or max; `do="c
       +lear"` for a new conversation; `do="compact"` to summarise its 
       +conversation. The lead can also say these straight to whoever h
       +as the floor ("plan mode", "think harder"), so use the tag when
       + they ask you about someone else ("put Juno in plan mode").    
    34  - `<call do="mute"/>` — the call itself: `end` hangs up, `hold`
         puts the call on hold, `pause` pauses it, `mute` mutes the lea
        d's microphone. Only when the lead asks. Each one stops you hea
        ring them, so before the tag say plainly what will happen and h
        ow they come back ("Muting you now. Press the microphone button
         to unmute."). Agents that are busy keep working through all of
         these.

        hey meant.

     4  
     5  The first message starts with a tag such as `[call · visual+aud
        io · you are on the line]`; it comes again only when it changes
        , so the last one you saw still holds. If the person cut in on 
        you, a `[cut in: …]` note says what they heard and what they mi
        ssed.
     6  
     7 +In plan mode you change nothing: you look around, write the pla
       +n, and hand it over with ExitPlanMode. The person reviews it on
       + screen and says yes or asks for changes, so out loud give only
       + a one-sentence summary and say it is ready for them ("The plan
       + is ready: three files, tests first. Have a look."). Once they 
       +approve, carry it out.                                         
     8 +                                                               
     9  If this conversation is ever summarised to make room, keep the

        is left and anything you are waiting on the person for. Leave o
        ut the call itself — tags, cut-ins, fillers, who was on the lin
        e.
    10  
    11  The tag also says whether you are **on the line** or **in the b
        ackground**. On the line, your `<say>` text is spoken aloud in 
        your own voice, right away. In the background it is not spoken 
        at all: the central assistant reads out a one-line summary inst
        ead. So when you are in the background, say less, and make the 
        last thing you say a single sentence that works on its own as a
         report — it is the only part the person will hear.

Agents now work in the installed app, and updates now actually take effect. You were right that the app and what I was testing didn't match, and that was my fault: none of my fixes had ever reached your installed app. What was wrong: - Every reinstall since 11:54 failed silently. When the app is installed over an existing setup, the install script quit early because of a small error, before restarting the background server. So the 11:54 server kept running all afternoon, without the fix for agents not being created. - My tests missed it because they always started from a fresh setup, never an update. - "Continue anyway" was a side effect. Replacing the app deleted the folder the old server was running from, so it could no longer start Claude Code and reported it as "not installed". - While fixing that, a second failure turned up. macOS refused to start the new server while the old one was still shutting down, which left nothing running at all. What's fixed: - The install script completes when your settings are kept, and waits for the old server to stop before starting the new one. - The launcher checks the running build after every install, and restarts the server if the old one is still up. If it can't, it tells you instead of pretending. - Auto-update: drag the new ClaudeCall over the old one, even while it's open. The open window notices it was replaced and relaunches as the new version, and open pages reload themselves when the server changes. If you're mid-call, the reload waits until the call ends. What I verified on your installed app, not a test copy: - I replaced the app while it was open, and the new build was serving 17 seconds later, in a single window. - Claude Code shows as installed and signed in, so no "Continue anyway". - I asked Ada for an agent: Tess appeared in the team list 3 seconds later and wrote its file (a cat haiku). - Removing Tess asked for confirmation first, then removed it. I've deleted that test folder. Your app is running the latest build now; open ClaudeCall to try it. The current DMG is in dist/. Everything is committed locally (c668c76) and all 199 tests pass.

You can interrupt Ada again, and her actions still don't drop. Your app is updated: I replaced it while it was open, and it was running the new version 18 seconds later. All 201 tests pass. The "New Agents forming" commit is done (cb95c4a). It's an empty checkpoint commit, because everything was already committed in c668c76. This fix is aef9794 on top of it. Why you couldn't interrupt. When I fixed the dropped actions earlier, I made talking over a busy Ada go to her as a side question (Claude Code's /btw) instead of stopping her. Nothing got lost, but she never actually stopped, so it felt like she ignored you. How it works now: - Talking over Ada cuts her off for real, and nothing is dropped. Anything she had already set up still happens (starting an agent, handing out work). If you cut in while she was in the middle of writing one, the same message that interrupts her tells her it didn't happen, so she redoes it. - A "switch you to Juno" she'd queued is skipped once you cut in, since you were just talking to her, and she's told. - Project agents busy with real work still get your words as a side question, so talking never kills their work, as you asked before. - Her voice gives way straight away. On speakers, it drops to a whisper the moment you start talking, then stops once your words are in, or comes back up if it was only noise or her own echo. With the Bluetooth switch on (headphones), she stops instantly. Tested live: five seconds into a slow weather lookup, I cut in with "make an agent called Bo". Ada stopped the lookup, and Bo was on the team 2.4 seconds later. Not tested: I can't try the voice dip with a real microphone myself. It only works with the Mac voices, which are on by default ("Voices placed left and right" in Settings). With the browser's built-in voice instead, she keeps talking at full volume until your words are in.

Good thing I tested it: the Mac-built .deb fails to install ("dpkg-deb: error: paste subprocess was killed"). Looking at what Debian's own tools make of the file:
Ran 1 shell command
The archive is structurally fine — the problem is that macOS's tar adds an extended LIBARCHIVE.creationtime header dpkg can't parse. I'll switch to the plain ustar format to avoid extended headers.

Turn 23

did you make a website with the 3 links ? users won't need to add an API key right? this completely runs on the claude subscription right ?
Claude's response
A website with the download links: no, I haven't made one yet. The README links to the GitHub Releases page, and that page is still empty until a version tag is pushed. An API key: users don't need one. Every agent, Ada included, is a Claude Code session on their computer, and it uses whatever Claude Code is signed in with. If someone has run claude auth login with a Claude Pro or Max subscription, ClaudeCall runs on that subscription. Pasting an API key is only an alternative on the setup screen, for people without a subscription. Speech recognition (Whisper) and the voices run on the computer itself, so there's nothing else to pay for. Two caveats: - Your Mac doesn't use a subscription. Its Claude Code is signed in through Google Vertex, so I haven't actually run ClaudeCall with a Claude subscription login. It should work the same, since ClaudeCall only starts claude and leaves sign-in to it, but that path is untested. - It uses the subscription's limits faster. Each agent is its own Claude Code session, and several can work at once, so a busy team hits the plan's usage limits sooner than one chat would. Max holds up better than Pro for this. For the website, I'd make a one-page download site in the same style as the app: Mac, Ubuntu/Debian (Intel/AMD) and Ubuntu/Debian (ARM), with a line about needing Claude Code. GitHub Pages can host it for free from this repository. Three things it needs that are your call, because they make things public: 1. Push a release tag (v0.1.0) so the files exist to link to. 2. Turn on GitHub Pages for the repository. 3. Upload each release's files under fixed names as well, so the links always point to the newest version without editing the page. Want me to go ahead with all three?
✻ Baked for 37s · done 8:38 AM

Turn 24

is the html page holding the 3 links ready ? the 3 links would point to the files in my repo right ?
Claude's response
It isn't ready: no such page exists yet. And to be precise about the links, they wouldn't point at files in your repository's code. The DMG and .deb files aren't stored in git (dist/ is ignored, and 50 MB binaries don't belong there). They'd point at files attached to a GitHub Release of your repo, which is where downloads normally live. The links would look like: https://github.com/palakraisinghani/TalkToClaude/releases/latest/download/Clau deCall-mac-apple-silicon.dmg That form always gives the newest release, as long as each release also carries a copy of each file under that fixed name. v0.1.0 is live with all three download links returning 200, but the x64 .deb is 155 MB versus 49 MB on Mac (ARM matches at 43 MB) — likely onnxruntime-node pulling in CUDA libraries on the Linux x64 build. I'll investigate that along with the APK, which shows as "0 MB".
Ran 1 shell command
The APK's small size is normal, but the x64 bloat comes from onnxruntime-node pulling in unused NVIDIA CUDA libraries (~100 MB). I'll fix this by having the installer skip them and also stripping any GPU libraries during the trimming step as a backup.

The release also has the .tar.xz files for other Linux distributions and the Android APK (33 KB; it's tiny because it only opens your computer's ClaudeCall). The x64 .deb is too big. On GitHub's x64 Linux machine, the speech recogniser's runtime downloaded about 100 MB of NVIDIA GPU libraries that ClaudeCall never uses, since speech recognition runs on the CPU. It still works, it's just a bigger download. The fix is committed locally (6d1b80c): the build skips those libraries and strips any that slip through. Anyone with these links can download: - Mac: https://github.com/palakraisinghani/TalkToClaude/releases/latest/downlo ad/ClaudeCall-mac-apple-silicon.dmg - Ubuntu/Debian (Intel/AMD): https://github.com/palakraisinghani/TalkToClaude/ releases/latest/download/ClaudeCall-linux-x64.deb - Ubuntu/Debian (ARM): https://github.com/palakraisinghani/TalkToClaude/releas es/latest/download/ClaudeCall-linux-arm64.deb Because of latest, the same links always give the newest release, so you won't need to send new ones after an update. ✻ Cooked for 2m 24s · done 9:09 AM

Turn 27

On installation and before opening it says Apple could not verify if its safe and fre of malware. How can I change thath ?
Claude's response
That's macOS Gatekeeper. Every app downloaded from the internet gets that warning unless two things are true: 1. It's signed with an Apple Developer ID certificate. Today the app is only "ad-hoc" signed, meaning signed without any identity. 2. It's notarized: uploaded to Apple, scanned automatically, and given a "ticket" that's attached to the DMG. Both require the Apple Developer Program, which costs $99 a year. There's no free way to remove the warning for other people's Macs. To fix it properly: 1. Join at developer.apple.com/programs, as Palak, individually. Approval usually takes a day or two. 2. Create a "Developer ID Application" certificate in Xcode or on the developer site, and export it as a .p12 file with a password. 3. Create an app-specific password for the Apple ID at appleid.apple.com. 4. Add these as secrets in the GitHub repository: - the certificate (as text) and its password - the Apple ID, the Team ID, and that app-specific password After that, every release DMG would be signed and notarized automatically, and the warning disappears for everyone. I can prepare the build and CI changes now, so they switch on by themselves once those secrets exist. Until then nothing changes. The changes are: signing every program inside the app with Apple's "hardened runtime" (needed for notarization), then submitting the DMG to Apple and attaching the ticket. One caveat is that I can't test notarization without the account, so the first signed release may need a fix-up round. Until then, here's what users can do. The README says this, briefly. I'd make it clearer, because the steps differ by macOS version: - macOS 15 (Sequoia) and later: open ClaudeCall once, and close the warning. Then go to System Settings → Privacy & Security, scroll down to "ClaudeCall was blocked…" and click Open Anyway. - macOS 14 and earlier: right-click ClaudeCall → Open → Open. - Or in Terminal: xattr -dr com.apple.quarantine /Applications/ClaudeCall.app Either way, it's only needed once per Mac. Want me to prepare the signing set-up, and update the README and download page with these clearer steps?
